I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or depressed. It's... well, my mood is generally fine. It's really just that constant worrying and the distraction I mentioned. I don't feel down, just preoccupied most of the time, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that triggers this worrying or does it seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn't really feel like there's one specific trigger... it's more that my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things. Like, I'm getting closer to stepping away from work, so I constantly worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or if the practice handoff is actually financially sound. And then I start worrying about whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. It just bounces around all these different things, and... honestly, the amount of worry feels out of proportion even to me, but I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Is this constant worrying affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work, I've actually had to slow down and... well, postpone parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing my decisions, and I end up losing a lot of time at the clinic because my mind is elsewhere. And it's not just at the office. Even at home, my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to. It's like I can't just sit and do them anymore without getting bogged down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like tremors, headaches, or gastrointestinal issues alongside your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't get tremors, and my stomach is fine. No headaches either. It's really just the worrying itself that's the problem. Physically, I feel pretty normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one area of your life, like work or retirement, or does it cover a wide range of topics and areas?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it covers a pretty wide range. It's definitely heavy on the retirement and the practice right now, like I mentioned, but... it's not just that. It feels like it just latches onto whatever is in front of me on any given day. I haven't really kept track of every single topic I worry about, to be honest, but I know it definitely bleeds into a lot more than just my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this excessive worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for a while now. I'd say it really started when I began the formal retirement process... which was about a year and three months ago.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this constant worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it is. It's... well, it's just exhausting, really. I don't know if I'd use the exact word distress, but it's definitely bothersome. It just takes up a lot of mental energy having my mind going all the time about the practice and everything else. So, yes, it's definitely a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Most nights I get into bed feeling pretty tired, but... as soon as the house is quiet, my mind just starts going. I start replaying patient transfers, looking over the money details in my head. And even when I do manage to get to sleep, I'm usually up at four or five in the morning, feeling like I never really settled.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, what finally got me to come in is that my concentration has just gotten really unreliable lately. I'll be at work—I'm a dentist—reviewing a patient's chart at the clinic, or even just at home trying to lay out a watercolor wash, and... I'll suddenly realize I've read the same line three times. My mind just wanders off to everything that could possibly go wrong. It's getting too distracting, so I figured it was time to see someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or depressed. It's... well, my mood is generally fine. It's really just that constant worrying and the distraction I mentioned. I don't feel down, just preoccupied most of the time, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that triggers this worrying or does it seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn't really feel like there's one specific trigger... it's more that my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things. Like, I'm getting closer to stepping away from work, so I constantly worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or if the practice handoff is actually financially sound. And then I start worrying about whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. It just bounces around all these different things, and... honestly, the amount of worry feels out of proportion even to me, but I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Is this constant worrying affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work, I've actually had to slow down and... well, postpone parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing my decisions, and I end up losing a lot of time at the clinic because my mind is elsewhere. And it's not just at the office. Even at home, my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to. It's like I can't just sit and do them anymore without getting bogged down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like tremors, headaches, or gastrointestinal issues alongside your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't get tremors, and my stomach is fine. No headaches either. It's really just the worrying itself that's the problem. Physically, I feel pretty normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one area of your life, like work or retirement, or does it cover a wide range of topics and areas?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it covers a pretty wide range. It's definitely heavy on the retirement and the practice right now, like I mentioned, but... it's not just that. It feels like it just latches onto whatever is in front of me on any given day. I haven't really kept track of every single topic I worry about, to be honest, but I know it definitely bleeds into a lot more than just my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this excessive worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for a while now. I'd say it really started when I began the formal retirement process... which was about a year and three months ago.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this constant worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it is. It's... well, it's just exhausting, really. I don't know if I'd use the exact word distress, but it's definitely bothersome. It just takes up a lot of mental energy having my mind going all the time about the practice and everything else. So, yes, it's definitely a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Most nights I get into bed feeling pretty tired, but... as soon as the house is quiet, my mind just starts going. I start replaying patient transfers, looking over the money details in my head. And even when I do manage to get to sleep, I'm usually up at four or five in the morning, feeling like I never really settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I worry a lot, and I feel keyed up, but I wouldn't say I get those sudden... intense bursts of fear like you're describing. It's more of just a constant, steady stream of worrying rather than a sudden peak of panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you feel an overwhelming sense of fear or anxiety that comes on quickly?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. It's just a constant loop of all these different things, and honestly, the amount I worry about it all feels out of proportion, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you don't worry about?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. It feels like everything is fair game, honestly. I mean, if there's something going on in my life, I'm probably finding a way to worry about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.8, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, what finally got me to come in is that my concentration has just gotten really unreliable lately. I'll be at work—I'm a dentist—reviewing a patient's chart at the clinic, or even just at home trying to lay out a watercolor wash, and... I'll suddenly realize I've read the same line three times. My mind just wanders off to everything that could possibly go wrong. It's getting too distracting, so I figured it was time to see someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or depressed. It's... well, my mood is generally fine. It's really just that constant worrying and the distraction I mentioned. I don't feel down, just preoccupied most of the time, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that triggers this worrying or does it seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn't really feel like there's one specific trigger... it's more that my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things. Like, I'm getting closer to stepping away from work, so I constantly worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or if the practice handoff is actually financially sound. And then I start worrying about whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. It just bounces around all these different things, and... honestly, the amount of worry feels out of proportion even to me, but I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Is this constant worrying affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work, I've actually had to slow down and... well, postpone parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing my decisions, and I end up losing a lot of time at the clinic because my mind is elsewhere. And it's not just at the office. Even at home, my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to. It's like I can't just sit and do them anymore without getting bogged down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like tremors, headaches, or gastrointestinal issues alongside your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't get tremors, and my stomach is fine. No headaches either. It's really just the worrying itself that's the problem. Physically, I feel pretty normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one area of your life, like work or retirement, or does it cover a wide range of topics and areas?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it covers a pretty wide range. It's definitely heavy on the retirement and the practice right now, like I mentioned, but... it's not just that. It feels like it just latches onto whatever is in front of me on any given day. I haven't really kept track of every single topic I worry about, to be honest, but I know it definitely bleeds into a lot more than just my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this excessive worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for a while now. I'd say it really started when I began the formal retirement process... which was about a year and three months ago.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this constant worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it is. It's... well, it's just exhausting, really. I don't know if I'd use the exact word distress, but it's definitely bothersome. It just takes up a lot of mental energy having my mind going all the time about the practice and everything else. So, yes, it's definitely a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Most nights I get into bed feeling pretty tired, but... as soon as the house is quiet, my mind just starts going. I start replaying patient transfers, looking over the money details in my head. And even when I do manage to get to sleep, I'm usually up at four or five in the morning, feeling like I never really settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I worry a lot, and I feel keyed up, but I wouldn't say I get those sudden... intense bursts of fear like you're describing. It's more of just a constant, steady stream of worrying rather than a sudden peak of panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you feel an overwhelming sense of fear or anxiety that comes on quickly?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I was just saying, it's not a sudden thing for me. It's just that constant, nagging worry in the background all the time. It's just a constant loop of all these different things, and honestly, the amount I worry about it all feels out of proportion, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you don't worry about?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. It feels like everything is fair game, honestly. I mean, if there's something going on in my life, I'm probably finding a way to worry about it.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I can't think of a time like that. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, what finally got me to come in is that my concentration has just gotten really unreliable lately. I'll be at work—I'm a dentist—reviewing a patient's chart at the clinic, or even just at home trying to lay out a watercolor wash, and... I'll suddenly realize I've read the same line three times. My mind just wanders off to everything that could possibly go wrong. It's getting too distracting, so I figured it was time to see someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or depressed. It's... well, my mood is generally fine. It's really just that constant worrying and the distraction I mentioned. I don't feel down, just preoccupied most of the time, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that triggers this worrying or does it seem to happen randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn't really feel like there's one specific trigger... it's more that my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things. Like, I'm getting closer to stepping away from work, so I constantly worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or if the practice handoff is actually financially sound. And then I start worrying about whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. It just bounces around all these different things, and... honestly, the amount of worry feels out of proportion even to me, but I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Is this constant worrying affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. At work, I've actually had to slow down and... well, postpone parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing my decisions, and I end up losing a lot of time at the clinic because my mind is elsewhere. And it's not just at the office. Even at home, my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to. It's like I can't just sit and do them anymore without getting bogged down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like tremors, headaches, or gastrointestinal issues alongside your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I don't get tremors, and my stomach is fine. No headaches either. It's really just the worrying itself that's the problem. Physically, I feel pretty normal.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one area of your life, like work or retirement, or does it cover a wide range of topics and areas?\n\nPatient: Well, I think it covers a pretty wide range. It's definitely heavy on the retirement and the practice right now, like I mentioned, but... it's not just that. It feels like it just latches onto whatever is in front of me on any given day. I haven't really kept track of every single topic I worry about, to be honest, but I know it definitely bleeds into a lot more than just my work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this excessive worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for a while now. I'd say it really started when I began the formal retirement process... which was about a year and three months ago.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this constant worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it is. It's... well, it's just exhausting, really. I don't know if I'd use the exact word distress, but it's definitely bothersome. It just takes up a lot of mental energy having my mind going all the time about the practice and everything else. So, yes, it's definitely a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping due to your worrying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Most nights I get into bed feeling pretty tired, but... as soon as the house is quiet, my mind just starts going. I start replaying patient transfers, looking over the money details in my head. And even when I do manage to get to sleep, I'm usually up at four or five in the morning, feeling like I never really settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I worry a lot, and I feel keyed up, but I wouldn't say I get those sudden... intense bursts of fear like you're describing. It's more of just a constant, steady stream of worrying rather than a sudden peak of panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you feel an overwhelming sense of fear or anxiety that comes on quickly?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I was just saying, it's not a sudden thing for me. It's just that constant, nagging worry in the background all the time. I don't get those quick, overwhelming waves of fear.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say so. I mean, my wife knows I'm stressed out a lot, especially with the practice, but it hasn't caused any real problems between us. And with my friends, things are fine. I mostly just keep all that worry in my own head, so it doesn't really spill over into my relationships with anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing that you worry about more than anything else?\n\nPatient: Not really just one thing, no. It's... well, my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things. I'm stepping back from my dental practice, so I worry a lot about whether my old patients are being looked after properly. And the practice handoff itself—I keep stressing over whether that's actually financially sound. \n\nAnd then... I don't know, I just worry about whether I’ll become useless once I’m fully retired. It's just a constant loop of all these different things, and honestly, the amount I worry about it all feels out of proportion, even to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you don't worry about?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. It feels like everything is fair game, honestly. I mean, if there's something going on in my life, I'm probably finding a way to worry about it.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I can't think of a time like that. It's just... always there, buzzing in the background, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I avoid things. I mean, I still go into the practice to help with the transition, and I still do what I need to do every day. I just... I carry the worry with me while I do it all. It doesn't really stop me from doing things, it just makes getting through them a lot more exhausting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
